[James Monroe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Monroe "James Monroe") ([1817–1825](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_James_Monroe "Presidency of James Monroe"))


## Demographics and other characteristics
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States&action=edit&section=23 "Edit section: Demographics and other characteristics")]
The Founding Fathers represented the upper echelon of political leadership in the British colonies during the latter half of the 18th century.[[260]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-261)[[261]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-262) All were leaders in their communities and respective colonies who were willing to assume responsibility for public affairs.[[262]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-263)
Of the signers of the Declaration of Independence, Articles of Confederation, and U.S. Constitution, nearly all were native born and of British heritage, including Scots, Irish, and Welsh.[[263]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-264)[[264]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-265) Nearly half were lawyers, while the remainder were primarily businessmen and planter-farmers.[[265]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-WertherAnalyzing-266)[[266]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-267)[[267]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-268) The average age of the founders was 43.[[268]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-269) Benjamin Franklin, born in 1706, was the oldest, while only a few were born after 1750 and thus were in their 20s.[[269]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-270)[[270]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-271)[[271]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-272)
The following sections discuss these and other demographic topics in greater detail. For the most part, the information is confined to signers/delegates associated with the Declaration of Independence, Articles of Confederation, and Constitution. 
### Political experience
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States&action=edit&section=24 "Edit section: Political experience")]
All of the Founding Fathers had extensive political experience at the national and state levels.[[272]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-273)[[273]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-274) As just one example, the signers of the Declaration of Independence and Articles of Confederation were members of Second Continental Congress, while four-fifths of the delegates at the Constitutional Convention had served in the Congress either during or prior to the convention. The remaining fifth attending the convention were recognized as leaders in the state assemblies that appointed them. 
Following are brief profiles of the political backgrounds of some of the more notable founders: 
  * John Adams began his political career as a town council member in [Braintree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Braintree,_Massachusetts "Braintree, Massachusetts") outside Boston. He came to wider attention following a series of essays he wrote during the Stamp Act crisis of 1765. In 1770, he was elected to the [Massachusetts General Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massachusetts_General_Assembly "Massachusetts General Assembly"), went on to lead Boston's Committee of Correspondence, and in 1774, was elected to the Continental Congress. Adams later became the first vice president (1789–1797) and second president (1797–1801) of the nation he helped found.[[274]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-275)[[275]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-276)
  * John Dickinson was one of the leaders of the Pennsylvania Assembly during the 1770s. As a member of the First and Second Continental Congress, he wrote two petitions for the Congress to King George III seeking a peaceful solution. Dickinson opposed independence and refused to sign the Declaration of Independence, but served as an officer in the militia and wrote the initial draft of the Articles of Confederation. In the 1780s, he served as [president of Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_governors_of_Pennsylvania "List of governors of Pennsylvania") and [president of Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_governors_of_Delaware "List of governors of Delaware") and as a delegate to the Constitutional Convention.[[276]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-277)
  * Benjamin Franklin retired from his business activities in 1747 and was elected to the [Pennsylvania Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania_Assembly "Pennsylvania Assembly") in 1751. He was sent to London in 1757 for the first of two diplomatic missions on behalf of the colony.[[277]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-278) Upon returning from England in 1775, Franklin was elected to the Second Continental Congress. After signing the Declaration of Independence in 1776, he was appointed Minister to France and then Sweden, and in 1783 helped negotiate the Treaty of Paris. Franklin was [governor of Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_governors_of_Pennsylvania "List of governors of Pennsylvania") from 1785 to 1788 and was a delegate to the Constitutional Convention.[[278]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-279)
  * John Jay was a New York delegate to the First and Second Continental Congress and in 1778 was elected [Congress president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_the_Continental_Congress "President of the Continental Congress"). In 1782, he was summoned to Paris by Franklin to help negotiate the Treaty of Paris with Great Britain. As a supporter of the proposed Constitution, he wrote five of the Federalist Papers and became the first [chief justice of the Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Justice_of_the_United_States "Chief Justice of the United States") following the Constitution's adoption.[[279]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-280) Minister to Spain[[2]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-morris1-2)[[280]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-jillson77-281)[[281]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-282)
  * [Thomas Jefferson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson "Thomas Jefferson") was a delegate from Virginia to the Second Continental Congress (1775–1776) and was the primary author of the Declaration of Independence. He was elected the [second governor of Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_governors_of_Virginia "List of governors of Virginia") (1779–1781) and served as Minister to France (1785–1789). He later served as the first secretary of state (1790–1793), second vice president (1797–1801) and third president of the United States (1801–1809)[[282]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-283)[[283]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-284)
  * Robert Morris had been a member of the [Pennsylvania Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania_Assembly "Pennsylvania Assembly") and president of Pennsylvania's [Committee of Safety](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committees_of_safety_\(American_Revolution\) "Committees of safety \(American Revolution\)"). He was also a member of the [Committee of Secret Correspondence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_of_Secret_Correspondence "Committee of Secret Correspondence") and member of the Second Continental Congress. Under the Articles of Confederation he served as the minister of finance and served as a delegate to the Constitutional Convention.[[284]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-285)
  * Roger Sherman had served in the First and Second Continental Congresses, [Connecticut House of Representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Connecticut_House_of_Representatives "Connecticut House of Representatives") and Justice of the Peace before attending the Constitutional Convention as a delegate. After the Constitution was ratified he served in both the U.S. House of Representatives and the U.S. Senate representing his home state of Connecticut. He was the only Founder to sign all four of the major founding documents, the Continental Association, Declaration of Independence, Articles of Confederation, and the U.S. Constitution.[[285]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-286)


### Education
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States&action=edit&section=25 "Edit section: Education")]
More than a third of the Founding Fathers attended or graduated from colleges in the American colonies, while additional founders attended college abroad, primarily in England and Scotland. All other founders either were home schooled, received tutoring, completed apprenticeships, or were self-educated. 
#### American institutions
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States&action=edit&section=26 "Edit section: American institutions")]
Following is a listing of founders who graduated from six of the nine colleges established in the Americas during the Colonial Era. A few founders, such as Alexander Hamilton[[286]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-287) and James Monroe,[[287]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-288) attended college (Columbia and William & Mary, respectively) but did not graduate. The other three colonial colleges, all founded in the 1760s, included [Brown University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown_University "Brown University") (College of Rhode Island), [Dartmouth College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dartmouth_College "Dartmouth College"), and [Rutgers University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rutgers_University "Rutgers University") (Queen's College). 
  * [College of William & Mary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_of_William_%26_Mary "College of William & Mary"): Thomas Jefferson,[[288]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-289) John Blair Jr.,[[289]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-BlairArchive-290) James McClurg,[[290]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-BioMcClurg-291) James Francis Mercer,[[291]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-292) Edmund Randolph[[292]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-293)
  * [Columbia University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbia_University "Columbia University") (King's College): John Jay,[[293]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-294) Robert R. Livingston,[[294]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-295) Gouverneur Morris,[[295]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-296)
  * [Harvard University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvard_University "Harvard University") (Harvard College): John Adams,[[296]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-297) Samuel Adams,[[297]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-298) Francis Dana,[[298]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-299) William Ellery,[[299]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-300) Elbridge Gerry,[[300]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-301) John Hancock,[[301]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-302) William Hooper,[[302]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-303) William Samuel Johnson (also Yale),[[303]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-BioJohnson-304) Rufus King,[[304]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-305) James Lovell,[[305]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-306) Robert Treat Paine,[[306]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-307) Caleb Strong,[[307]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-308) Joseph Warren,[[308]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-BioWarren-309) John Wentworth Jr.,[[309]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-310) William Williams[[310]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-311)
  * [Princeton University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princeton_University "Princeton University") (The College of New Jersey):[[311]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-312) Gunning Bedford Jr.,[[312]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-313) William Richardson Davie,[[313]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-314) Jonathan Dayton,[[314]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-315) Oliver Ellsworth,[[315]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-316) Joseph Hewes,[[316]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-317) William Houstoun,[[317]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-318) Richard Hutson,[[318]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-319) James Madison,[[319]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-320) Alexander Martin,[[320]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-321) Luther Martin,[[321]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-322) William Paterson,[[322]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-323) Joseph Reed,[[323]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-BioReed-324) Benjamin Rush,[[324]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-BioRush-325) Nathaniel Scudder,[[325]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-BioScudder-326) Jonathan Bayard Smith,[[326]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-327) Richard Stockton[[327]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-328)
  * [University of Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Pennsylvania "University of Pennsylvania") ([College of Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_and_College_of_Philadelphia "Academy and College of Philadelphia")): Francis Hopkinson,[[328]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-329) Henry Marchant,[[329]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-330) Thomas Mifflin,[[330]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-331) William Paca,[[331]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-BioPaca-332) Hugh Williamson[[332]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-BioWilliamson-333)
  * [Yale University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_University "Yale University") (Yale College): Andrew Adams,[[333]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-334) Abraham Baldwin,[[334]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-BioHall-335) Lyman Hall,[[335]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-336) Titus Hosmer,[[336]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-337) Jared Ingersoll,[[337]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-338) William Samuel Johnson (also Harvard),[[303]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-BioJohnson-304) Philip Livingston,[[338]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-339) William Livingston,[[339]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-340) Lewis Morris,[[340]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-341) Oliver Wolcott[[341]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-342)


#### British institutions
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States&action=edit&section=27 "Edit section: British institutions")]
Following are founders who graduated from institutions in Britain: 
  * [Inner Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inner_Temple "Inner Temple"), is one of the four [Inns of Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inns_of_Court "Inns of Court") in London offering legal studies for admission to the English Bar: William Houstoun,[[342]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-auto3-343) William Paca (also University of Pennsylvania graduate)[[343]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-The_North_Carolina_Review-344)[[331]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-BioPaca-332)
  * [Middle Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Temple "Middle Temple"), also one of the four Inns of Court: John Banister,[[344]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-345) John Blair,[[345]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-ArchivesBlair-346) John Dickinson,[[346]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-BioDickinson-347) Thomas Heyward Jr.,[[347]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-auto-348) Thomas Lynch Jr. (also University of Cambridge graduate),[[347]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-auto-348) John Matthews,[[348]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-349) Charles Cotesworth Pinckney,[[342]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-auto3-343) Peyton Randolph,[[343]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-The_North_Carolina_Review-344)[[349]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-BioRandolph-350) John Rutledge[[342]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-auto3-343)
  * [University of Cambridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Cambridge "University of Cambridge"), England: Thomas Lynch Jr. (also Middle Temple graduate),[[350]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-351) Thomas Nelson Jr.[[351]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-352)
  * [University of Edinburgh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Edinburgh "University of Edinburgh"), Scotland: Benjamin Rush,[[352]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-353) John Witherspoon[[353]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-BioWitherspoon-354)


### Ethnicity
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States&action=edit&section=28 "Edit section: Ethnicity")]
All of the founders were white, and two-thirds (36 out of 55) were natives of the American Colonies, while nineteen were born in other parts of the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire#"First"_British_Empire_\(1707%E2%80%931783\) "British Empire"). 
  * England: William Richardson Davie,[[354]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-355) William Duer,[[355]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-356) Button Gwinnett,[[356]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-357) Robert Morris,[[357]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-358) Thomas Paine[[358]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-359)
  * Ireland: Pierce Butler,[[359]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-malone364-365-360) Thomas Fitzsimons,[[360]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-fitz444-361) James McHenry,[[361]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-auto4-362) William Paterson,[[362]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-363) James Smith,[[363]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-364) George Taylor,[[364]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-BioTaylor-365) Charles Thomson,[[365]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-366) Matthew Thornton[[366]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-367)
  * Scotland: Edward Telfair,[[367]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-368) James Wilson,[[368]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-369) John Witherspoon[[369]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-370)
  * Wales: Francis Lewis[[370]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-BioLewis-371)
  * West Indies: Alexander Hamilton,[[371]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-372) Daniel Roberdeau[[372]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-373)


### Occupations
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States&action=edit&section=29 "Edit section: Occupations")]
While the Founding Fathers were engaged in a broad range of occupations, most had careers in three professions: about half the founders were lawyers, a sixth were planters/farmers, another sixth were merchants/businessmen, and the others were spread across miscellaneous professions. 
  * Ten founders were physicians: Josiah Bartlett,[[373]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-BioBartlett-374) Lyman Hall,[[334]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-BioHall-335) Samuel Holten,[[374]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-BioHolten-375) James McClurg,[[290]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-BioMcClurg-291) James McHenry (surgeon),[[375]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-376) Benjamin Rush,[[324]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-BioRush-325) Nathaniel Scudder,[[325]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-BioScudder-326) Matthew Thornton,[[376]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-Thornton-377) Joseph Warren,[[308]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-BioWarren-309) and Hugh Williamson.[[332]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-BioWilliamson-333)
  * John Witherspoon was the only minister, although Lyman Hall had been a preacher prior to becoming a physician.[[377]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-Witherspoon-378)[[334]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-BioHall-335)
  * George Washington, a Virginia planter, was a land surveyor before becoming a colonel in the [Virginia Regiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Regiment "Virginia Regiment").[[378]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-379)
  * Benjamin Franklin was a successful printer and publisher and an accomplished scientist and inventor, in Philadelphia. Franklin retired at age 42 to focus first on scientific pursuits and then politics and diplomacy, serving as a member of the Continental Congress, first postmaster general, minister to Great Britain, France, and Sweden, and governor of Pennsylvania.[[379]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-BioFranklin-380)[[380]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-FranklinExperience-381)[[381]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-382)[[382]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-383)


## Religion
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States&action=edit&section=30 "Edit section: Religion")]
See also: [First Great Awakening](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Great_Awakening "First Great Awakening"), [Religious views of George Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_views_of_George_Washington "Religious views of George Washington"), and [Religious views of Thomas Jefferson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_views_of_Thomas_Jefferson "Religious views of Thomas Jefferson")
Of the 55 delegates to the [Constitutional Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Convention_\(United_States\) "Constitutional Convention \(United States\)") in 1787, 28 were [Anglicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglicanism "Anglicanism") ([Church of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_England "Church of England") or [Episcopalian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Episcopal_Church_\(United_States\) "Episcopal Church \(United States\)")), 21 were other [Protestants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestants "Protestants"), and three were [Catholics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church "Catholic Church") (Daniel Carroll and Fitzsimons; Charles Carroll was Catholic but was not a Constitution signatory).[[383]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-auto9-384) Among the non-Anglican Protestant delegates to the Constitutional Convention, eight were [Presbyterians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presbyterianism "Presbyterianism"), seven were [Congregationalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congregational_church "Congregational church"), two were [Lutherans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lutheranism "Lutheranism"), two were [Dutch Reformed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_Reformed_Church "Dutch Reformed Church"), and two were [Methodists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methodism "Methodism").[[383]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-auto9-384)
A few prominent Founding Fathers were [anti-clerical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-clericalism "Anti-clericalism"), notably Jefferson.[[384]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-385)[[385]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-386) Many founders deliberately avoided public discussion of their faith. Historian [David L. Holmes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_L._Holmes "David L. Holmes") uses evidence gleaned from letters, government documents, and second-hand accounts to identify their religious beliefs.[[48]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-auto10-49)
## Founders on currency and postage
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States&action=edit&section=31 "Edit section: Founders on currency and postage")]
Four U.S. founders are minted on [American currency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_currency "American currency")—[Benjamin Franklin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Franklin "Benjamin Franklin"), [Alexander Hamilton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Hamilton "Alexander Hamilton"), [Thomas Jefferson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson "Thomas Jefferson"), and [George Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington "George Washington"); Washington and Jefferson both appear on three different denominations. Additionally, the reverse of Jefferson's two-dollar bill features [John Trumbull's 1818 depiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_Independence_\(painting\) "Declaration of Independence \(painting\)") of the signing of the Declaration of Independence. 
Founding Father name  | Currency image  | Denomination   
---|---|---  
**[George Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington "George Washington")** |  [![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/70/2021-P_US_Quarter_Obverse.jpg/120px-2021-P_US_Quarter_Obverse.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:2021-P_US_Quarter_Obverse.jpg) |  [Quarter dollar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quarter_\(United_States_coin\) "Quarter \(United States coin\)") (quarter)  
25¢

## Political and cultural impact
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States&action=edit&section=32 "Edit section: Political and cultural impact")]
See also: [Commemoration of the American Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commemoration_of_the_American_Revolution "Commemoration of the American Revolution"), [Independence Day (United States)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Day_\(United_States\) "Independence Day \(United States\)"), and [Legacy of George Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legacy_of_George_Washington "Legacy of George Washington")
### Political rhetoric
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States&action=edit&section=33 "Edit section: Political rhetoric")]
According to David Sehat, in modern politics:[[386]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-387)
> Everyone cites the Founders. Constitutional originalists consult the Founders' papers to decide original meaning. Proponents of a living and evolving Constitution turn to the Founders as the font of ideas that have grown over time. Conservatives view the Founders as architects of a free enterprise system that built American greatness. The more liberal-leaning, following their sixties parents, claim the Founders as egalitarians, suspicious of concentrations of wealth. Independents look to the Founders to break the logjam of partisan brinksmanship. Across the political spectrum, Americans ground their views in a supposed set of ideas that emerged in the eighteenth century. But, in fact, the Founders disagreed with each other....they had vast and profound differences. They argued over federal intervention in the economy and about foreign policy. They fought bitterly over how much authority rested with the executive branch, about the relationship and prerogatives of federal and state government. The Constitution provided a nearly limitless theater of argument. The founding era was, in reality, one of the most partisan periods of American history.
### Holidays
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States&action=edit&section=34 "Edit section: Holidays")]
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/Fourth_of_July_fireworks_behind_the_Washington_Monument%2C_1986.jpg/250px-Fourth_of_July_fireworks_behind_the_Washington_Monument%2C_1986.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Fourth_of_July_fireworks_behind_the_Washington_Monument,_1986.jpg)[Fireworks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fireworks "Fireworks"), such as these shown over the [Washington Monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Monument "Washington Monument") in Washington, D.C. on July 4, 1986, are an annual national holiday tradition every July 4 in celebration of [Independence Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Day_\(United_States\) "Independence Day \(United States\)") and the founding of the United States.
[Independence Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Day_\(United_States\) "Independence Day \(United States\)") (colloquially called the _Fourth of July_) is a United States national holiday celebrated yearly on July 4 to commemorate the signing of the Declaration of Independence and the founding of the nation. [Washington's Birthday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President%27s_Day "President's Day") is also observed as a national federal holiday, and on April 13 [Jefferson's Birthday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jefferson%27s_Birthday "Jefferson's Birthday") honors the US founder and president. 
### Media and theater
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States&action=edit&section=35 "Edit section: Media and theater")]
See also: [List of plays and musicals about the American Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_plays_and_musicals_about_the_American_Revolution "List of plays and musicals about the American Revolution"), [List of films about the American Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_films_about_the_American_Revolution "List of films about the American Revolution"), and [List of television series and miniseries about the American Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_television_series_and_miniseries_about_the_American_Revolution "List of television series and miniseries about the American Revolution")
The Founding Fathers were portrayed in the [Tony Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Award "Tony Award")–winning 1969 musical [_1776_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1776_\(musical\) "1776 \(musical\)"), which depicted the debates over and eventual adoption of the Declaration of Independence. The stage production was adapted into the [1972 film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1776_\(film\) "1776 \(film\)") of the same name. The 1989 film _[A More Perfect Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_More_Perfect_Union_\(film\) "A More Perfect Union \(film\)")_ , which was filmed on location in [Independence Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Hall "Independence Hall"), depicts the events of the Constitutional Convention. The writing and passing of the founding documents are depicted in the 1997 documentary miniseries _[Liberty!](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberty! "Liberty!")_ , and the passage of the Declaration of Independence is portrayed in the second episode of the 2008 miniseries _[John Adams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Adams_\(miniseries\)#Part_II:_Independence_\(1774%E2%80%931776\) "John Adams \(miniseries\)")_ and the third episode of the 2015 miniseries _[Sons of Liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sons_of_Liberty_\(miniseries\) "Sons of Liberty \(miniseries\)")_. The Founders also feature in the 1986 miniseries _[George Washington II: The Forging of a Nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington_II:_The_Forging_of_a_Nation "George Washington II: The Forging of a Nation")_ , the 2002–2003 animated television series _[Liberty's Kids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberty%27s_Kids "Liberty's Kids")_ , the 2020 miniseries _[Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_\(miniseries\) "Washington \(miniseries\)")_ , and in [many other films](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_films_about_the_American_Revolution "List of films about the American Revolution") and [television portrayals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_television_series_and_miniseries_about_the_American_Revolution "List of television series and miniseries about the American Revolution").[_[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_]
Several Founding Fathers, Hamilton, Washington, Jefferson, and Madison—were reimagined in [_Hamilton_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamilton_\(musical\) "Hamilton \(musical\)"), a 2015 [musical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musical_theater "Musical theater") inspired by [Ron Chernow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ron_Chernow "Ron Chernow")'s 2004 biography [_Alexander Hamilton_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Hamilton_\(book\) "Alexander Hamilton \(book\)"), with music, lyrics and book by [Lin-Manuel Miranda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lin-Manuel_Miranda "Lin-Manuel Miranda"). The musical won eleven Tony Awards and a [Pulitzer Prize for Drama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulitzer_Prize_for_Drama "Pulitzer Prize for Drama").[[387]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-388)
### Sports
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States&action=edit&section=36 "Edit section: Sports")]
Several major professional sports teams in the Northeastern United States are named for themes based on the founders: 
  * [New England Patriots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_England_Patriots "New England Patriots") ([National Football League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Football_League "National Football League"))
  * [New England Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_England_Revolution "New England Revolution") ([Major League Soccer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_League_Soccer "Major League Soccer"))
  * [New York Liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Liberty "New York Liberty") ([Women's National Basketball Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women%27s_National_Basketball_Association "Women's National Basketball Association"))
  * [Philadelphia 76ers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_76ers "Philadelphia 76ers") ([National Basketball Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Basketball_Association "National Basketball Association"))
  * [Washington Capitals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Capitals "Washington Capitals") ([National Hockey League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Hockey_League "National Hockey League"))
  * [Washington Nationals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Nationals "Washington Nationals") ([Major League Baseball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_League_Baseball "Major League Baseball"))


## Religious freedom
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States&action=edit&section=37 "Edit section: Religious freedom")]
Religious persecution had existed for centuries around the world and it existed in colonial America.[[388]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-gelfand7-389) Founders such as [Thomas Jefferson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson "Thomas Jefferson"), [James Madison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Madison "James Madison"), [Patrick Henry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patrick_Henry "Patrick Henry"), and [George Mason](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Mason "George Mason") first established a measure of religious freedom in [Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia "Virginia") in 1776 with the [Virginia Declaration of Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Declaration_of_Rights "Virginia Declaration of Rights"), which became a model for religious liberty for the nation.[[389]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-auto1-390) Prior to this, [Baptists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptists "Baptists"), [Presbyterians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presbyterianism "Presbyterianism"), and [Lutherans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lutheranism "Lutheranism") had for a decade petitioned against the [Church of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_England "Church of England")'s efforts to suppress religious liberties in Virginia. 
Jefferson left the Continental Congress to return to Virginia to join the fight for religious freedom, which proved difficult since many members of the Virginia legislature belonged to the established Church of England. While Jefferson was not completely successful, he managed to have repealed the various laws that were punitive toward those with different religious beliefs.[[389]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-auto1-390)[[390]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-391)[[391]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-meacham123-392) Jefferson was the architect for [separation of Church and State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Separation_of_church_and_state_in_the_United_States "Separation of church and state in the United States"), which opposed the use of public funds to support any established religion and believed it was unwise to link civil rights to religious doctrine.[[392]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-393)[[391]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-meacham123-392)
The United States Constitution, ratified in 1788, states in Article VI that "no religious Test shall ever be required as a Qualification to any Office or public Trust under the United States". [Freedom of religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_religion_in_the_United_States "Freedom of religion in the United States") and [freedom of speech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_speech_in_the_United_States "Freedom of speech in the United States") were further affirmed as the nation's law in the [Bill of Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Bill_of_Rights "United States Bill of Rights").[[388]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-gelfand7-389) The [14th Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourteenth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution "Fourteenth Amendment to the United States Constitution") of 1868 provided all Americans with "equal protection under the laws" and thus applied the First Amendment restriction against limiting the free exercise of religion to the states.[[393]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-394)[[394]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-395)
Washington, a local leader of the Church of England, was also a strong proponent of religious freedom. He assured Baptists worried that the Constitution might not protect their religious liberties, that, "... certainly, I would never have placed my signature to it." Jews also viewed Washington as a champion of freedom and sought his assurances that they would enjoy complete religious freedom. Washington responded by declaring America's revolution in religion stood as an example for the rest of the world.[[395]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-396)
## Slavery
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States&action=edit&section=38 "Edit section: Slavery")]
Main article: [Slavery and the United States Founding Fathers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_and_the_United_States_Founding_Fathers "Slavery and the United States Founding Fathers")
Further information: [Slavery in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_United_States "Slavery in the United States"), [George Washington and slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington_and_slavery "George Washington and slavery"), [Thomas Jefferson and slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson_and_slavery "Thomas Jefferson and slavery"), and [James Madison and slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Madison_and_slavery "James Madison and slavery")
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1a/George_Washington_by_John_Trumbull_%281780%29.jpg/250px-George_Washington_by_John_Trumbull_%281780%29.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:George_Washington_by_John_Trumbull_\(1780\).jpg)_[George Washington and William Lee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington_\(Trumbull\) "George Washington \(Trumbull\)")_ , a 1780 portrait by [John Trumbull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Trumbull "John Trumbull")
The Founding Fathers were not unified on the issue of slavery and continued to accommodate it within the new nation. Some were morally opposed to it and some attempted to end it in several of the colonies, but nationally, slavery remained protected. In her study of Jefferson, a slaveholder of 600 slaves, [Annette Gordon-Reed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annette_Gordon-Reed "Annette Gordon-Reed") notes ironically, "Others of the founders held slaves, but no other founder drafted the charter for American freedom".[[396]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-reed2000-397) As well as Jefferson, Washington and many other Founding Fathers were slaveowners; 41 of the 56 signers of the Declaration owned slaves. Some were conflicted by the institution, seeing it as immoral and politically divisive; Washington freed his slaves, in his will. Jay and Hamilton led the successful fight to outlaw the international slave trade in New York, with efforts beginning in 1777.[[397]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-398)[[398]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-399) Thomas Jefferson included an anti-slavery clause in his original draft of the Declaration of Independence:[[399]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-400)
> He [King George] has waged cruel war against human nature itself, violating its most sacred rights of life and liberty in the persons of a distant people who never offended him, captivating & carrying them into slavery in another hemisphere or to incur miserable death in their transportation thither. This piratical warfare, the opprobrium of infidel powers, is the warfare of the Christian King of Great Britain. Determined to keep open a market where Men should be bought & sold, he has prostituted his negative for suppressing every legislative attempt to prohibit or restrain this execrable commerce. And that this assemblage of horrors might want no fact of distinguished die, he is now exciting those very people to rise in arms among us, and to purchase that liberty of which he has deprived them, by murdering the people on whom he has obtruded them: thus paying off former crimes committed again the Liberties of one people, with crimes which he urges them to commit against the lives of another. 
Founders such as Samuel Adams and John Adams were against slavery. Rush wrote a pamphlet in 1773 which criticized the slave trade, and slavery. Rush argued scientifically that Africans are not intellectually or morally inferior, and any apparent evidence to the contrary is only the "perverted expression" of slavery, which "is so foreign to the human mind, that the moral faculties, as well as those of the understanding are debased, and rendered torpid by it." The Continental Association contained a clause which banned any [Patriot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriot_\(American_Revolution\) "Patriot \(American Revolution\)") involvement in slave trading.[[400]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-401)[[401]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-402)[[402]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-403)[[403]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-404)
Franklin, though a key founder of the [Pennsylvania Abolition Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania_Abolition_Society "Pennsylvania Abolition Society"),[[404]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-Wright_p125-405) owned slaves whom he [manumitted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manumission "Manumission") (released). While serving in the Rhode Island Assembly, in 1769 Hopkins introduced one of the earliest anti-slavery laws in the colonies. When Jefferson entered public life as a member of the House of Burgesses, he began as a social reformer by an effort to secure legislation permitting emancipation of slaves. Jay founded the [New York Manumission Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Manumission_Society "New York Manumission Society") in 1785, for which Hamilton became an officer. They and other members of the Society founded the [African Free School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Free_School "African Free School") in New York, to educate the children of free blacks and slaves. When Jay was governor of New York in 1798, he helped secure and signed into law an abolition law; fully ending forced labor as of 1827. He freed his slaves in 1798. Hamilton opposed slavery, as his experiences left him familiar with it and its effect on slaves and slaveholders,[[405]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-406) though he did negotiate slave transactions for his wife's family, the [Schuylers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schuyler_family "Schuyler family").[[406]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-407) Evidence suggests Hamilton may have owned a house slave[_[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_] and after the [Jay Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jay_Treaty "Jay Treaty") was signed, Hamilton advocated that American slaves freed by the British during the war be forcibly returned to their enslavers.[[407]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-408)[[408]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-409) Henry Laurens, ran the largest slave trading house in North America. In the 1750s alone, his firm, Austin and Laurens, handled sales of more than 8,000 Africans.[[409]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-Brown-410)
[Slaves and slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_United_States "Slavery in the United States") are mentioned indirectly in the 1787 Constitution. For example, [Article 1, Section 2, Clause 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three-fifths_Compromise "Three-fifths Compromise") prescribes that "three-fifths of all other Persons" are to be counted for the apportionment of seats in the [House of Representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_House_of_Representatives "United States House of Representatives") and direct taxes. Additionally, in [Article 4, Section 2, Clause 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fugitive_Slave_Clause "Fugitive Slave Clause"), slaves are referred to as "persons held in service or labor".[[404]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-Wright_p125-405)[[410]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-Freehling_TAHR_p85-411) The Founding Fathers made some efforts to contain slavery. Many Northern states had adopted legislation to end, or significantly reduce slavery, during and after the revolution.[[410]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-Freehling_TAHR_p85-411) In 1782, Virginia passed a manumission law that allowed owners to free their slaves by will or deed.[[411]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-Cambridge_p278-412) As a result, thousands of slaves were manumitted in Virginia.[[411]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-Cambridge_p278-412) In the [Ordinance of 1784](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ordinance_of_1784 "Ordinance of 1784"), Jefferson proposed to ban slavery in all the western territories, which failed to pass Congress by one vote. Partially following Jefferson's plan, Congress did ban slavery in the [Northwest Ordinance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwest_Ordinance "Northwest Ordinance"), for lands north of the [Ohio River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ohio_River "Ohio River"). The [international slave trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_slave_trade "Atlantic slave trade") was banned in all states except South Carolina by 1800. In 1807, President Jefferson called for and signed into law a federally enforced ban on the international slave trade, throughout the U.S. and its territories. It became a federal crime to import or export a slave. However, the domestic slave trade was allowed for expansion or for diffusion of slavery into the [Louisiana Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Territory "Louisiana Territory").[[410]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-Freehling_TAHR_p85-411)
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States&action=edit&section=39 "Edit section: Reconstruction as a "Second Founding"")]
Further information: [Reconstruction Amendments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconstruction_Amendments "Reconstruction Amendments") and [African American founding fathers of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_American_founding_fathers_of_the_United_States "African American founding fathers of the United States")
According to [Jeffrey K. Tulis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeffrey_K._Tulis "Jeffrey K. Tulis") and Nicole Mellow:[[412]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-413)
> The Founding, Reconstruction (often called "the second founding"), and the New Deal are typically heralded as the most significant turning points in the country's history, with many observers seeing each of these as political triumphs through which the United States has come to more closely realize its liberal ideals of liberty and equality.
Scholars such as [Eric Foner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eric_Foner "Eric Foner") have expanded the theme.[[413]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-414)[[414]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-415)[[415]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-416) Black abolitionists played a key role by stressing that freed blacks needed equal rights after slavery was abolished.[[416]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-417) Biographer [David Blight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Blight "David Blight") states that [Frederick Douglass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederick_Douglass "Frederick Douglass"), "played a pivotal role in America's Second Founding out of the apocalypse of the Civil War, and he very much wished to see himself as a founder and a defender of the Second American Republic."[[417]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-418) Constitutional provision for racial equality for free blacks was enacted by a Republican Congress led by [Thaddeus Stevens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thaddeus_Stevens "Thaddeus Stevens"), [Charles Sumner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Sumner "Charles Sumner") and [Lyman Trumbull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyman_Trumbull "Lyman Trumbull").[[418]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-419) The "second founding" comprised the [13th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirteenth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution "Thirteenth Amendment to the United States Constitution"), [14th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourteenth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution "Fourteenth Amendment to the United States Constitution") and [15th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fifteenth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution "Fifteenth Amendment to the United States Constitution") amendments to the Constitution. All citizens now had federal rights that could be enforced in federal court. In a deep reaction, after 1876 freedmen lost many of these rights and had second class citizenship in the era of lynching and [Jim Crow laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Crow_laws "Jim Crow laws"). Finally in the 1950s the U.S., Supreme Court started to restore those rights. Under the leadership of [Martin Luther King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Luther_King "Martin Luther King") and [James Bevel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Bevel "James Bevel"), the [Civil Rights movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_movement "Civil Rights movement") made the nation aware of the crisis, and under [President Lyndon Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Lyndon_B._Johnson "Presidency of Lyndon B. Johnson") major civil rights legislation was passed in 1964–65, and 1968.[[419]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-420)
## Scholarly analysis
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States&action=edit&section=40 "Edit section: Scholarly analysis")]
Main article: [Historiography of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historiography_of_the_United_States "Historiography of the United States")
There are thousands of historians who have written about the [American Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolution "American Revolution") era and the founding of the United States government. Some of the most prominent ones are listed below. While most scholarly works maintain overall objectivity, historian Arthur H. Shaffer notes that many of the early works about the American Revolution often express a national bias, or anti-bias. Shaffer maintains that this bias lends a direct insight into the minds of the founders and their adversaries respectively. He notes that any bias is the product of a national interest and prevailing political mood, and as such cannot be dismissed as having no historic value for the modern historian.[[420]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-421) Conversely, various modern accounts of history contain [anachronisms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anachronisms "Anachronisms"), modern day ideals and perceptions used in an effort to write about the past and as such can distort the historical account in an effort to placate a modern audience.[[421]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-422)[[422]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-423)
### Early historians
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States&action=edit&section=41 "Edit section: Early historians")]
Several of the earliest histories of the founding of the United States and its founders were written by [Jeremy Belknap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeremy_Belknap "Jeremy Belknap"), author of his three-volume work, _The history of New-Hampshire_ , published in 1784.[[423]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-424)
  * [Henry Adams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Adams "Henry Adams"), grandson of [John Quincy Adams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Quincy_Adams "John Quincy Adams"), wrote a nine-volume work, _The History of the United States of America During the Administrations of Thomas Jefferson and James Madison_ , which is acclaimed for its literary style, documentary evidence, and first-hand knowledge of major figures during the early [Revolutionary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolution "American Revolution") era.[[424]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-425)
  * [Rufus Wilmot Griswold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rufus_Wilmot_Griswold "Rufus Wilmot Griswold") authored _Washington and the Generals of the Revolution_ , a two-volume work, in 1885.
  * [Albert Bushnell Hart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Bushnell_Hart "Albert Bushnell Hart"), a [Harvard University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvard_University "Harvard University") history professor, edited a 27-volume work, _The American Nation: A History_ , published in 1904–1918.[[425]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-426)
  * [John Marshall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Marshall "John Marshall"), a [U.S. Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Supreme_Court "United States Supreme Court") Justice, published a two-volume biography of [Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington "George Washington") in 1832, three years before his death.
  * [David Ramsay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Ramsay_\(historian\) "David Ramsay \(historian\)") is regarded as one of the first major historians of the [American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War "American Revolutionary War").[[426]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-Cooney-427)
  * [Mercy Otis Warren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercy_Otis_Warren "Mercy Otis Warren"), who wrote extensively about the Revolution and post-Revolution eras, published all her works anonymously until 1790.[[426]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-Cooney-427)[[427]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-428)
  * [Mason Locke Weems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mason_Locke_Weems "Mason Locke Weems") authored the first biography of Washington in 1800, which includes the famed story about a young Washington cutting down a cherry tree.[[428]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-429)
  * [William Wirt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Wirt_\(Attorney_General\) "William Wirt \(Attorney General\)") wrote the first biography on [Patrick Henry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patrick_Henry "Patrick Henry") in 1805, but was accused for excessive praise of Henry.[[429]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-430)


### Modern historians
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States&action=edit&section=42 "Edit section: Modern historians")]
Articles and books by these and other 20th- and 21st-century historians, combined with the digitization of primary sources such as handwritten letters, continue to contribute to an encyclopedic body of knowledge about the Founding Fathers: 
  * [Ron Chernow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ron_Chernow "Ron Chernow") won the [Pulitzer Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulitzer_Prize "Pulitzer Prize") for his 2010 [biography of Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington:_A_Life "Washington: A Life"). His 2004 [bestselling book _Alexander Hamilton_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Hamilton_\(book\) "Alexander Hamilton \(book\)") inspired the 2015 blockbuster [musical of the same name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamilton_\(musical\) "Hamilton \(musical\)").
  * [Douglas Southall Freeman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_Southall_Freeman "Douglas Southall Freeman") wrote an extensive seven volume biography on [Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington "George Washington"). Historian and George Washington biographer [John E. Ferling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_E._Ferling "John E. Ferling") maintains that no other biography for Washington compares to that of Freeman's work.[[430]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-431)
  * [Dumas Malone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dumas_Malone "Dumas Malone") is noted for his six-volume biography _Jefferson and His Time_ , for which he received the 1975 Pulitzer Prize, and for his co-editorship of the 20-volume _[Dictionary of American Biography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dictionary_of_American_Biography "Dictionary of American Biography")_.


  * [Annette Gordon-Reed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annette_Gordon-Reed "Annette Gordon-Reed") is an American historian and [Harvard Law School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvard_Law_School "Harvard Law School") professor. She is noted for changing scholarship on Jefferson regarding his alleged relationship with [Sally Hemings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sally_Hemings "Sally Hemings") and her children. She has studied the challenges faced by the Founding Fathers, particularly as it relates to their position and actions on slavery.[[396]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-reed2000-397)
  * [Jack P. Greene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jack_P._Greene "Jack P. Greene") is an American historian who specializes in colonial-era American history.
  * [David McCullough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_McCullough "David McCullough")'s [Pulitzer Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulitzer_Prize "Pulitzer Prize")–winning 2001 book _[John Adams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Adams_\(book\) "John Adams \(book\)")_ focuses on [Adams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Adams "John Adams"), and his 2005 book, _[1776](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1776_\(book\) "1776 \(book\)")_ details Washington's military history in the American Revolution and other independence events carried out by America's founders.[[431]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-432)
  * [Peter S. Onuf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_S._Onuf "Peter S. Onuf") and [Jack N. Rakove](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jack_N._Rakove "Jack N. Rakove") have researched Jefferson extensively.


According to American historian [Joseph Ellis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Ellis "Joseph Ellis"), the concept of the Founding Fathers of the U.S. emerged in the 1820s as the last survivors died out. Ellis says the founders, or the fathers comprised an aggregate of semi-sacred figures whose particular accomplishments and singular achievements were decidedly less important than their sheer presence as a powerful but faceless symbol of past greatness. For the generation of national leaders coming of age in the 1820s and 1830s, such as [Andrew Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Jackson "Andrew Jackson"), [Henry Clay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Clay "Henry Clay"), [Daniel Webster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Webster "Daniel Webster"), and [John C. Calhoun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_C._Calhoun "John C. Calhoun"), the founders represented heroic but anonymous abstraction whose long shadow fell across all followers and whose legendary accomplishments defied comparison.[_[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_]
> We can win no laurels in a war for independence. Earlier and worthier hands have gathered them all. Nor are there places for us ... [as] the founders of states. Our fathers have filled them. But there remains to us a great duty of defence and preservation. 
[Daniel Webster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Webster "Daniel Webster"), 1825[[432]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States#cite_note-433)
### Noted collections
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States&action=edit&section=43 "Edit section: Noted collections")]
  * [Adams Papers Editorial Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adams_Papers_Editorial_Project "Adams Papers Editorial Project"), an ongoing project by the Massachusetts Historical Society to organize, transcribe, and documents authored by and by the family of [John Adams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Adams "John Adams"), his wife [Abigail Adams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abigail_Adams "Abigail Adams"), and their family, including [John Quincy Adams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Quincy_Adams "John Quincy Adams")
  * [Founders Online](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founders_Online "Founders Online"), a searchable database of over 184,000 documents authored by or addressed to George Washington, John Jay, Benjamin Franklin, John Adams (and family), Thomas Jefferson, Alexander Hamilton, and James Madison
  * [The Papers of Benjamin Franklin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Papers_of_Benjamin_Franklin "The Papers of Benjamin Franklin") at [Yale University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_University "Yale University")
  * [The Papers of James Madison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Papers_of_James_Madison "The Papers of James Madison") at the [University of Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Virginia "University of Virginia")
  * [The Papers of Thomas Jefferson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Papers_of_Thomas_Jefferson "The Papers of Thomas Jefferson") at [Princeton University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princeton_University "Princeton University")
  * [The Selected Papers of John Jay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Selected_Papers_of_John_Jay "The Selected Papers of John Jay") at [Columbia University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbia_University "Columbia University")
  * [The Washington Papers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Washington_Papers "The Washington Papers") at the University of Virginia

  * Squiers, Anthony. "The Apotheosis of the Founding Fathers and Signs of Filial Piety". in _The Politics of the Sacred in America: The Role of Civil Religion in Political Practice_ (2018) pp: 75–96.

---  
  * [3rd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_presidents_of_the_United_States "List of presidents of the United States") [President of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_the_United_States "President of the United States") (1801–1809)
  * [2nd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_vice_presidents_of_the_United_States "List of vice presidents of the United States") [Vice President of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vice_President_of_the_United_States "Vice President of the United States") (1797–1801)
  * [1st](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_secretaries_of_state "List of United States secretaries of state") [United States Secretary of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_State "United States Secretary of State") (1790–1793)

[James Monroe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Monroe "James Monroe")  
---  
  * [5th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_presidents_of_the_United_States "List of presidents of the United States") [President of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_the_United_States "President of the United States") (1817–1825)
  * [5th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_secretaries_of_state "List of United States secretaries of state") [United States Secretary of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_State "United States Secretary of State") (1811–1817)
  * [United States Secretary of War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_War "United States Secretary of War") (1814–1815)
  * [12th and 16th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_governors_of_Virginia "List of governors of Virginia") [Governor of Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_of_Virginia "Governor of Virginia") (1799–1802, 1811)
  * [U.S. Minister to the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_ambassadors_of_the_United_States_to_the_United_Kingdom "List of ambassadors of the United States to the United Kingdom") (1803–1808)
  * [U.S. Minister to France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_ambassadors_of_the_United_States_to_France "List of ambassadors of the United States to France") (1794–1796)
  * [U.S. Senator from Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_senators_from_Virginia "List of United States senators from Virginia") (1790–1794)
  * [Delegate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_delegates_to_the_Continental_Congress "List of delegates to the Continental Congress") to the [Congress of the Confederation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_of_the_Confederation "Congress of the Confederation") (1783–1786)
